What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’ve just been feeling really down lately. Not in a dramatic way, I don’t think, but I wake up with this heavy, kind of hollow sadness. Like… the studio lights are on, but there’s no music in me anymore, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a while, I guess. Maybe around nine months? I kept thinking it was just a rough patch or I’d shake it off once things got less busy, but it’s sort of stayed there. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like before this period of feeling down began?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I still like the idea of the things I used to enjoy, if that makes sense. Like dance, music, being in the studio — those are still important to me. I just don’t always feel the same spark right now. But maybe that’s normal when you’re tired or in a low patch?\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in the activities you're doing, even if you don't feel the same spark?\n\nPatient: Some, I think. Like… I can still appreciate that something is beautiful, or I can feel glad I showed up to rehearsal. It’s just quieter than it used to be. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m completely numb or anything, because I’m not sure that would be fair. It’s more like the pleasure is there, but kind of muted? Is that… an okay way to describe it?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now you don't find any pleasure in them at all?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s anything that’s gone completely flat, no. At least… not that I can clearly point to. Some things take more effort, and I don’t always get as much out of them as I used to, but I wouldn’t want to say there’s zero pleasure. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling down?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… not dramatic or anything, I don’t think. When I was really buzzing with ideas, I’d forget to eat or skip meals without meaning to. And now it’s more like food just doesn’t taste like much — kind of cardboard-y — so I’m not eating as much. My rehearsal clothes have been hanging a bit looser than usual. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of feeling down like this before?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’ve had low days before, sure, but this feels more noticeable. What brings me in is that I’ve been waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness… like the studio lights are on, but there’s no music in me anymore. That sounds a little dramatic, I know, but it’s the closest way I can explain it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any significant worries or fears lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them significant, honestly. I mean, I worry a bit about whether I’m functioning well enough, or if I’m making too much of this, but… not like a specific fear that’s taken over. At least I don’t think so. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you previously found pleasurable but now find you have no interest in at all?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “no interest at all.” That sounds so absolute. I still… I still care about the things I usually like, at least in theory. They just feel harder to start, or less rewarding once I’m doing them.\n\nLike with dance-related things, I can make myself show up, but it doesn’t always have that spark. But I wouldn’t want to overstate it — I’m not completely uninterested. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities where you've completely lost the ability to feel pleasure?\n\nPatient: Completely lost the ability… no, I don’t think so. I mean, there are moments where things feel muted, like I’m going through the motions more than really enjoying them, but I can still feel little bits of pleasure here and there.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. It’s more like the volume is turned down, not totally gone. Is that the kind of distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt this kind of persistent sadness or loss of interest?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe not more than one like this. What brought me in now is that I’ve been waking up with this kind of heavy, hollow sadness. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic — like the studio lights are on, but there’s no music in me anymore.\n\nI’m sorry, that probably sounds a little poetic. But that’s the closest I can get to it. I’m not sure if I’ve had repeated periods exactly like this before.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this kind of sadness or loss of interest before this current period?\n\nPatient: Before this… not really in the same way, I don’t think. I mean, I’ve had sad days like anyone, or times where I felt off, but this current thing is different enough that it’s what made me come in.\n\nIt’s that waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness — like everything is set up for me to move, but there’s no music in me. I’m sorry, I know that sounds a little… theatrical. But no, I don’t think I’ve felt it quite like this before.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with depression before this current period?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I haven’t been told I had depression before, at least not formally.\n\nI’ve had rough patches, but nothing where someone diagnosed me with it. Does that answer it the way you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of depression similar to this one before in your life?\n\nPatient: Not really, no — not similar to this. I’ve had normal sadness before, but what brings me in now is waking up with this heavy, hollow feeling… like the studio lights are on, but there’s no music in me anymore.\n\nI hope that makes sense. This feels different from anything I remember before.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of feeling down or depressed like this before in your life that lasted for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can remember — not for two weeks like this. I’ve had sad days, of course, but this is the first time I’ve been waking up with that heavy, hollow sadness… like the studio lights are on but there’s no music in me anymore.\n\nI’m saying that okay, right? It just feels different from my usual ups and downs.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. But maybe that’s normal when you’re tired or in a low patch?\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in the activities you're doing, even if you don't feel the same spark?\n\nPatient: Some, I think. Like… I can still appreciate that something is beautiful, or I can feel glad I showed up to rehearsal. It’s just quieter than it used to be. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m completely numb or anything, because I’m not sure that would be fair. It’s more like the pleasure is there, but kind of muted? Is that… an okay way to describe it?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now you don't find any pleasure in them at all?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s anything that’s gone completely flat, no. At least… not that I can clearly point to. Some things take more effort, and I don’t always get as much out of them as I used to, but I wouldn’t want to say there’s zero pleasure. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling down?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… not dramatic or anything, I don’t think. When I was really buzzing with ideas, I’d forget to eat or skip meals without meaning to. And now it’s more like food just doesn’t taste like much — kind of cardboard-y — so I’m not eating as much. My rehearsal clothes have been hanging a bit looser than usual. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of feeling down like this before?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’ve had low days before, sure, but this feels more noticeable. What brings me in is that I’ve been waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness… like the studio lights are on, but there’s no music in me anymore. That sounds a little dramatic, I know, but it’s the closest way I can explain it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any significant worries or fears lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them significant, honestly. I mean, I worry a bit about whether I’m functioning well enough, or if I’m making too much of this, but… not like a specific fear that’s taken over. At least I don’t think so. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you previously found pleasurable but now find you have no interest in at all?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “no interest at all.” That sounds so absolute. I still… I still care about the things I usually like, at least in theory. They just feel harder to start, or less rewarding once I’m doing them.\n\nLike with dance-related things, I can make myself show up, but it doesn’t always have that spark. But I wouldn’t want to overstate it — I’m not completely uninterested. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities where you've completely lost the ability to feel pleasure?\n\nPatient: Completely lost the ability… no, I don’t think so. I mean, there are moments where things feel muted, like I’m going through the motions more than really enjoying them, but I can still feel little bits of pleasure here and there.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. It’s more like the volume is turned down, not totally gone. Is that the kind of distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt this kind of persistent sadness or loss of interest?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe not more than one like this. What brought me in now is that I’ve been waking up with this kind of heavy, hollow sadness. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic — like the studio lights are on, but there’s no music in me anymore.\n\nI’m sorry, that probably sounds a little poetic. But that’s the closest I can get to it. I’m not sure if I’ve had repeated periods exactly like this before.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this kind of sadness or loss of interest before this current period?\n\nPatient: Before this… not really in the same way, I don’t think. I mean, I’ve had sad days like anyone, or times where I felt off, but this current thing is different enough that it’s what made me come in.\n\nIt’s that waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness — like everything is set up for me to move, but there’s no music in me. I’m sorry, I know that sounds a little… theatrical. But no, I don’t think I’ve felt it quite like this before.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with depression before this current period?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I haven’t been told I had depression before, at least not formally.\n\nI’ve had rough patches, but nothing where someone diagnosed me with it. Does that answer it the way you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of depression similar to this one before in your life?\n\nPatient: Not really, no — not similar to this. I’ve had normal sadness before, but what brings me in now is waking up with this heavy, hollow feeling… like the studio lights are on, but there’s no music in me anymore.\n\nI hope that makes sense. This feels different from anything I remember before.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of feeling down or depressed like this before in your life that lasted for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can remember — not for two weeks like this. I’ve had sad days, of course, but this is the first time I’ve been waking up with that heavy, hollow sadness… like the studio lights are on but there’s no music in me anymore.\n\nI’m saying that okay, right? It just feels different from my usual ups and downs.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you previously enjoyed where you now feel almost no pleasure at all?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “almost no pleasure,” exactly… I mean, I still care about dance and music. It’s just maybe harder to get into it sometimes.\n\nBut I don’t want to overstate it. I can still appreciate things here and there. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m completely numb or anything, because I’m not sure that would be fair. It’s more like the pleasure is there, but kind of muted? Is that… an okay way to describe it?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now you don't find any pleasure in them at all?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s anything that’s gone completely flat, no. At least… not that I can clearly point to. Some things take more effort, and I don’t always get as much out of them as I used to, but I wouldn’t want to say there’s zero pleasure. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling down?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… not dramatic or anything, I don’t think. When I was really buzzing with ideas, I’d forget to eat or skip meals without meaning to. And now it’s more like food just doesn’t taste like much — kind of cardboard-y — so I’m not eating as much. My rehearsal clothes have been hanging a bit looser than usual. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of feeling down like this before?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’ve had low days before, sure, but this feels more noticeable. What brings me in is that I’ve been waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness… like the studio lights are on, but there’s no music in me anymore. That sounds a little dramatic, I know, but it’s the closest way I can explain it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any significant worries or fears lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them significant, honestly. I mean, I worry a bit about whether I’m functioning well enough, or if I’m making too much of this, but… not like a specific fear that’s taken over. At least I don’t think so. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you previously found pleasurable but now find you have no interest in at all?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “no interest at all.” That sounds so absolute. I still… I still care about the things I usually like, at least in theory. They just feel harder to start, or less rewarding once I’m doing them.\n\nLike with dance-related things, I can make myself show up, but it doesn’t always have that spark. But I wouldn’t want to overstate it — I’m not completely uninterested. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities where you've completely lost the ability to feel pleasure?\n\nPatient: Completely lost the ability… no, I don’t think so. I mean, there are moments where things feel muted, like I’m going through the motions more than really enjoying them, but I can still feel little bits of pleasure here and there.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. It’s more like the volume is turned down, not totally gone. Is that the kind of distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt this kind of persistent sadness or loss of interest?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe not more than one like this. What brought me in now is that I’ve been waking up with this kind of heavy, hollow sadness. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic — like the studio lights are on, but there’s no music in me anymore.\n\nI’m sorry, that probably sounds a little poetic. But that’s the closest I can get to it. I’m not sure if I’ve had repeated periods exactly like this before.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this kind of sadness or loss of interest before this current period?\n\nPatient: Before this… not really in the same way, I don’t think. I mean, I’ve had sad days like anyone, or times where I felt off, but this current thing is different enough that it’s what made me come in.\n\nIt’s that waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness — like everything is set up for me to move, but there’s no music in me. I’m sorry, I know that sounds a little… theatrical. But no, I don’t think I’ve felt it quite like this before.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with depression before this current period?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I haven’t been told I had depression before, at least not formally.\n\nI’ve had rough patches, but nothing where someone diagnosed me with it. Does that answer it the way you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of depression similar to this one before in your life?\n\nPatient: Not really, no — not similar to this. I’ve had normal sadness before, but what brings me in now is waking up with this heavy, hollow feeling… like the studio lights are on, but there’s no music in me anymore.\n\nI hope that makes sense. This feels different from anything I remember before.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of feeling down or depressed like this before in your life that lasted for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can remember — not for two weeks like this. I’ve had sad days, of course, but this is the first time I’ve been waking up with that heavy, hollow sadness… like the studio lights are on but there’s no music in me anymore.\n\nI’m saying that okay, right? It just feels different from my usual ups and downs.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you previously enjoyed where you now feel almost no pleasure at all?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “almost no pleasure,” exactly… I mean, I still care about dance and music. It’s just maybe harder to get into it sometimes.\n\nBut I don’t want to overstate it. I can still appreciate things here and there. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities where you feel no pleasure at all?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think there’s anything where I feel no pleasure at all. It’s more like it takes extra effort to feel connected to it, if that makes sense.\n\nI still love dance and music — I just maybe don’t get that same spark every time right now. But “no pleasure at all” sounds stronger than what I’d say. Is that an okay distinction?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Maybe around nine months? I kept thinking it was just a rough patch or I’d shake it off once things got less busy, but it’s sort of stayed there. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like before this period of feeling down began?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I still like the idea of the things I used to enjoy, if that makes sense. Like dance, music, being in the studio — those are still important to me. I just don’t always feel the same spark right now. But maybe that’s normal when you’re tired or in a low patch?\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in the activities you're doing, even if you don't feel the same spark?\n\nPatient: Some, I think. Like… I can still appreciate that something is beautiful, or I can feel glad I showed up to rehearsal. It’s just quieter than it used to be. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m completely numb or anything, because I’m not sure that would be fair. It’s more like the pleasure is there, but kind of muted? Is that… an okay way to describe it?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now you don't find any pleasure in them at all?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s anything that’s gone completely flat, no. At least… not that I can clearly point to. Some things take more effort, and I don’t always get as much out of them as I used to, but I wouldn’t want to say there’s zero pleasure. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling down?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… not dramatic or anything, I don’t think. When I was really buzzing with ideas, I’d forget to eat or skip meals without meaning to. And now it’s more like food just doesn’t taste like much — kind of cardboard-y — so I’m not eating as much. My rehearsal clothes have been hanging a bit looser than usual. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of feeling down like this before?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’ve had low days before, sure, but this feels more noticeable. What brings me in is that I’ve been waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness… like the studio lights are on, but there’s no music in me anymore. That sounds a little dramatic, I know, but it’s the closest way I can explain it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any significant worries or fears lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them significant, honestly. I mean, I worry a bit about whether I’m functioning well enough, or if I’m making too much of this, but… not like a specific fear that’s taken over. At least I don’t think so. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you previously found pleasurable but now find you have no interest in at all?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “no interest at all.” That sounds so absolute. I still… I still care about the things I usually like, at least in theory. They just feel harder to start, or less rewarding once I’m doing them.\n\nLike with dance-related things, I can make myself show up, but it doesn’t always have that spark. But I wouldn’t want to overstate it — I’m not completely uninterested. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities where you've completely lost the ability to feel pleasure?\n\nPatient: Completely lost the ability… no, I don’t think so. I mean, there are moments where things feel muted, like I’m going through the motions more than really enjoying them, but I can still feel little bits of pleasure here and there.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. It’s more like the volume is turned down, not totally gone. Is that the kind of distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt this kind of persistent sadness or loss of interest?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe not more than one like this. What brought me in now is that I’ve been waking up with this kind of heavy, hollow sadness. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic — like the studio lights are on, but there’s no music in me anymore.\n\nI’m sorry, that probably sounds a little poetic. But that’s the closest I can get to it. I’m not sure if I’ve had repeated periods exactly like this before.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this kind of sadness or loss of interest before this current period?\n\nPatient: Before this… not really in the same way, I don’t think. I mean, I’ve had sad days like anyone, or times where I felt off, but this current thing is different enough that it’s what made me come in.\n\nIt’s that waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness — like everything is set up for me to move, but there’s no music in me. I’m sorry, I know that sounds a little… theatrical. But no, I don’t think I’ve felt it quite like this before.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with depression before this current period?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I haven’t been told I had depression before, at least not formally.\n\nI’ve had rough patches, but nothing where someone diagnosed me with it. Does that answer it the way you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of depression similar to this one before in your life?\n\nPatient: Not really, no — not similar to this. I’ve had normal sadness before, but what brings me in now is waking up with this heavy, hollow feeling… like the studio lights are on, but there’s no music in me anymore.\n\nI hope that makes sense. This feels different from anything I remember before.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of feeling down or depressed like this before in your life that lasted for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can remember — not for two weeks like this. I’ve had sad days, of course, but this is the first time I’ve been waking up with that heavy, hollow sadness… like the studio lights are on but there’s no music in me anymore.\n\nI’m saying that okay, right? It just feels different from my usual ups and downs.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you previously enjoyed where you now feel almost no pleasure at all?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “almost no pleasure,” exactly… I mean, I still care about dance and music. It’s just maybe harder to get into it sometimes.\n\nBut I don’t want to overstate it. I can still appreciate things here and there. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities where you feel no pleasure at all?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think there’s anything where I feel no pleasure at all. It’s more like it takes extra effort to feel connected to it, if that makes sense.\n\nI still love dance and music — I just maybe don’t get that same spark every time right now. But “no pleasure at all” sounds stronger than what I’d say. Is that an okay distinction?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that this period of sadness is the worst you've ever experienced?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I don’t want to sound dramatic, but this waking up with that heavy, hollow sadness feels different from anything I remember. Like everything is technically there, but the music isn’t in me the same way.\n\nIs that the kind of “worst” you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m still functioning, mostly… like I’m not completely falling apart or anything. I do show up for the essential things.

But, if I’m being honest, I’m not functioning like I normally do. I’ve fallen behind on teaching prep, and I missed a grant deadline, which… I hate even saying that. My studio got really chaotic too, enough that collaborators started handling basic scheduling for me.

So I guess it is impairing things, just maybe not in a totally catastrophic way. Is that what you mean?

Is that an okay distinction?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that this period of sadness is the worst you've ever experienced?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I don’t want to sound dramatic, but this waking up with that heavy, hollow sadness feels different from anything I remember. I’ve fallen behind on teaching prep, and I missed a grant deadline, which… I hate even saying that. Is that what you mean?\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you describe your current sadness as mildly distressing or significantly impairing your daily life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / dancer_or_choreographer / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I fell behind on teaching prep and missed a grant deadline, and my studio got so chaotic that collaborators started handling basic scheduling for me. I’m still showing up for essentials, but I’m not functioning like I normally do as a choreographer or teacher.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’d probably say… more than mildly distressing, even if I keep wanting to make it sound manageable.

I’m still showing up for the essential things, so I don’t want to overstate it. But I fell behind on teaching prep, missed a grant deadline, and my studio got chaotic enough that collaborators started taking over basic scheduling. That’s not really normal for me as a choreographer or teacher.

So… yes, I guess it is significantly impairing my daily life. Is that the right way to put it?
